as a memorial to two of its deceased members, Professor ALBERT 
T. Cxiay, who died in September, 1925, and James B. NIEs, 
Ph.D., who died in June, 1922. The former originated the 
project of the Library, and obtained the codperation of its initial 
list of contributors; the latter gave to the Society a Publication 
Fund of $10,000, of which the income by vote of the Directors 
of the Society is devoted to the publication of the successive 
volumes of this series.

PREFACE 


THE preparation of this volume, begun in 1918, has been 
greatly prolonged by the pressure of other professional work as 
well as by administrative duties connected with the American 
Schools of Oriental Research. It was my intention from the 
time it was undertaken to dedicate it to my friend and co- 
laborer Professor Clay. As he has now passed from us, the 
execution of my purpose can only be accomplished by inscribing 
the volume to his memory. 

My gratitude is due to M. Thureau-Dangin for kindly fur- 
nishing me with squeezes of several unpublished inscriptions of 
Ur-Nina for use in the preparation of this book, and to all my 
predecessors whose labors of excavation, publication, and de- 
cipherment have made this volume possible. No matter how 
much one may at times differ from his predecessors in trans- 
lating, it is especially true of one who prepares such a book as 
this that ‘‘others have labored” and he ‘“‘enters into their 
labors.” : 

Especial thanks are due to the editorial committee, and also 
to my wife, for invaluable aid in reading the proofs. With 
their painstaking help numerous errors have been eliminated. 

GEORGE A. BARTON

INTRODUCTION 


THE inscriptions translated in this volume form the earliest his- 
torical records from ancient Babylonia, a country which contests 
with Egypt the honor of having produced the earliest civilization in 
the world. With our present knowledge it does not seem possible to 
decide between these two rivals. To the writer it seems probable 
that the emergence of what may be called a civilized culture in the 
two great river-valleys was nearly, if not quite, coeval. It is enough 
to say of Babylonia that it was the cradle of one of the earliest of the 
world’s civilizations. From the earliest times to about 2100 B.C. 
Babylonia was not politically a unified country; it was rather the 
home of various city-states, which were continually contending with 
one another. Again and again one of these cities, because of the 
energy and skill of an especially successful ruler, would reduce many 
or all of the other city states to vassalage and establish a short-lived 
empire. Occasionally it happened that such a ruler became the 
founder of a line of vigorous rulers which was able to maintain the 
leadership for three or four generations, but it happened more often 
that as soon as such a conqueror passed away the cities which he had 
subdued reasserted their independence and temporary unity gave 
way again to a congeries of petty states. In the Sumerian language, 
the oldest written language of the country, there are two general 
names for the land, kalam-ma, ‘‘the land” or ‘‘the country” and 
ki-en-gt, ‘‘the land producing reeds.”” From about 2450 B.C. onward, 
the country was divided into two parts; the southern portion was 
called Sumer, and the northern portion, Akkad. Akkad is obviously 
derived from the name of the city Agade, which furnished a dynasty 
which dominated the country for more than a century; the origin of 
the name Sumer is not so clear. It has been suspected that it arose 
from the name of Girsu, one of the boroughs of Lagash, written, as 
sometimes happens in very early Sumerian, in reverse order of the 
signs. This is, however, uncertain. 

During the historical period Babylonia was frequently subject to 
attack and invasion by the inhabitants of the less fertile contiguous 
lands. There is reason to believe that the same was true in pre- 


xvii 


XVili Introduction 


historic time. It has long been known that two races, the Sumerian 
and the Semitic, were in Babylonia before the dawn of history,! and 
the excavations of the last few years have brought to light a type of 
very early pottery? which makes it probable that a third race, 
kindred, perhaps, to the Elamites, had also been there. We are 
learning that every nation has been formed as the result of a ‘melting 
pot,” and the ancient Babylonians were no exception. 

Of these three or more races two, the Sumerian and Akkadian, as 
the Semitic element was called, dominated the country during the 
historical period. Of these the Sumerians were, during the earliest 
period, the more advanced in culture. They appear to have been 
the inventors of that pictographic writing out of which the cuneiform 
script was developed, and to have been the developers of that script 
during the earliest period of its history. The relations between 
Babylonia and Elam were, however, very close during this time, and. 
we find the cuneiform script employed also in Elam. There might 
seem in consequence to be some question as to whether the Elamites 
were not the inventors of the cuneiform writing. The Elamitic origin 
of the script cannot, however, be seriously considered, for, in the first 
place the earliest Elamitic system of writing contains signs which evi- 
dently originated in an alluvial country, like Babylonia, and, in the 
second place, the cuneiform system of writing was evolved from the 
primitive pictographs because clay was employed as a writing ma- 
terial, and clay is the product of the alluvium of Babylonia rather 
than of the mountain heights of Elam. 

Semites who, from time immemorial had trickled into the country 
from Arabia, gained possession, at some time during the fourth 


millennium before Christ, of the cities of Kish and Agade, and ~ 


about 2775 B.C. a Semitic dynasty arose in these cities, which lasted 
for 197 years and for more than a hundred years dominated not only 
all Babylonia, but Elam and many of the countries to the North and 
West. The inscriptions that were written before the rise of this

196 ff. and E. Meyer’s Sumerier und Semiten in Babylonien, 1906, 74 ff. 

2 Cf. R. C. Thompson, in Archaeologia, Miscellaneous Tracts Relating to An- 
tiquity, published by the Society of Antiquaries, vol. XX, 1920, 101-144; C. R. 
Hall, Proceedings of the Society of Antiquaries, London, 1919-20, pp. 22-44: 
Journal of Egyptian Archaeology, VIII, 1922, 241 ff.; IX, 1923, 177 ff.; also Langdon, 
Excavations at Kish, I, 67 £., and Expository Times, XXXVIII, 71 (Nov., 1926). 


Introduction xix 


dynasty were, in so far as they are known to us, practically all in the 
Sumerian language. The monarchs of this great dynasty began to 
write in Akkadian, often making their inscriptions bilingual, writing 
them in both Sumerian and Akkadian. About the time of the rule 
of this forceful line of monarchs inscriptions in Akkadian begin to 
appear not only in Babylonia, but in a number of the countries adjoin- 
ing it. The patesis and viceroys of the kings of Agade and Kish, in 
Elam, and in other countries wrote in Semitic, but so did Itur- 
Shamash, ruler of Kisurra, Anu-banini, king of Lulubu, some of the 
rulers of Ashnunnak, Der, Khurishtu, Gankhar, Urkish and Nawar, 
of the Guti and of the town that stood on the site of Sheichan. From 
this time onward both languages were employed, though the older 
associations connected with the Sumerian as the language of civiliza- 
tion and of religious culture led, in many instances, to the employ- 
ment of that tongue by Semites. A parallel to this is afforded by 
the employment of Latin by the peoples of western Europe in 
mediaeval and modern times. 

In Southern Babylonia Sumerian appears to have been a spoken 
language at least until the time of Hammurabi, about 2100 B.C.; in 
the North, Akkadian became the vulgar tongue. It was this in part 
that led to the recognition that Babylonia consisted of two sections, 
Sumer and Akkad. The racial connections of the Sumerians are as 
yet unknown to us, as is the name by which they originally designated 
themselves. If the conjecture mentioned above as to how the name 
Sumer originated is at all near the truth, the original name of the 
people who spoke what we call Sumerian must have been something 
quite different. 

It is the purpose of this volume to give translations of the royal 
inscriptions that were written in Sumerian and Akkadian anterior 
to the First Dynasty of Babylon. Excavations have been made at 
but a few of the sites where such monarchs reigned. Mugheir (Ur), 
Tell Obeid, Telloh, Nuffar, Bismya, Fara, Senkereh, Kish and dis- 
tant.Susa are the sites that have been in part searched for them. 
Fortunately many inscriptions of these rulers have been found out- 
side the places named, sometimes on the surface of the ground, but 
more often by the digging of natives. A comparison of the con- 
tents of the present volume with the contents of Thureau-Dangin’s 
Sumerischen und akkadischen Koénigsinschriften will show that in the 
last twenty years a large number of texts have been brought to light. 


XxX Introduction 


The number known to us is doubtless but a small fraction of the 
number that are still buried under the mounds of Babylonia and 
contiguous lands Valuable as these inscriptions are, there are, how- 
ever, such gaps in them that it is not yet possible from them to 
reconstruct a complete picture of the history of the country. That 
task awaits the recovery of many other such records which are still 
buried. 

An effort has been made in the following pages to arrange the ma- 
terial chronologically, but the gaps in our knowledge are such that 
this could not be wholly accomplished. ‘The dynastic tablet from 
Nippur discovered by Poebel,' together with the fragments added 
by Legrain,” that published by Scheil, and the chronological prism at 
Oxford published by Langdon® give us a view of the general suc- 
cession of dynasties in Babylonia that is doubtless approximately cor- 
rect.4. It may be that dynasties, which the Babylonian chronologers 
represented as successive, overlapped somewhat (as the dynasties 
of Isin and Larsa were practically contemporaneous throughout their 
history), but in general the outline given in these texts may be 
followed. In so far as those dynastic lists mention the rulers whose 
inscriptions this volume contains, that has been done. We have, 
however, inscriptions of rulers of Kish, Nippur, Surippak, Lagash, 
and many other places, of whom these tablets make no mention. 
Wherever we have any intimation of the succession of dynasties, 
either in the royal inscriptions themselves or elsewhere, it has been 
followed in a general way. Where, however, as in the case of Nippur 
and Susa, we have inscriptions from but a few scattered rulers, the 
first of whom lived at an early period, the others being scattered 
along at intervals through an undefined period of time, it has seemed 
better to group the translations together than to attempt a chrono- 
logical arrangement for the carrying out of which we have not the 
data. For example, the earliest ruler of Susa, whose inscription is in 
a Babylonian language, was contemporary with Manishtusu. The 
latest ruled shortly before the First Dynasty of Babylon near the end 
of the period covered by this book. It has, however, seemed best 
to group them all together, and their inscriptions have been placed at 


1UMBS, V, no. 2 (cf. UMBS, IV, 73-147). 

2 UMBS, XIII, nos. 1 and 2; cf. pp. 5-28. ¢ 

3 Oxford Editions of Cuneiform Texts, II, no. 1; cf. pp. 1-27. 
4 For a translation of these texts, see Appendix I. 


Introduction xxl 


the part of the volume where the first of these rulers would chronologi- 
cally belong. While the arrangement of the volume is in general 
chronological, it is in part geographical. In order that the geograph- 
ical arrangement might not prevail over the chronological the inscrip- 
tions of the rulers of Lagash were separated into two blocks, the pre- 
Sargonic rulers being placed in one group and the post-Sargonic in 
the other. Between these two groups the inscriptions of the Dynasty 
of Kish and Agade which Sargon founded have been placed, together 
with the inscriptions of rulers of various places, the earliest repre- 
sentatives of whom appear to have lived about the time of this 
dynasty, or between this dynasty and the time of Gudea. The ar- 
rangement is not altogether satisfactory, but, under the circum- 
stances, it seems the best that can be made. 

For the history of ancient Babylonia and contiguous countries the 
texts contained in this volume are of the highest importance, since, 
with the exception of a few texts in ancient Elamitic, they constitute 
our only source of information. Incomplete as they are, therefore, 
and however much may be added to them in the future, from the 
historical point of view it is difficult to overestimate their importance. 
It should be added, too, that for the history of the religion of ancient 
Babylonia they are scarcely less important. They are saturated with 
religious ideas and record many religious customs. Many of them 
deal with the building of temples and the presentation of votive 
offerings. The inscriptions of Urukagina record a social and religious 
reform; the cylinder inscriptions of Gudea take us into the mysteries 
of inspired visions, the ways of the gods, and the mystic connection 
between the gods and nature. As a source of information concern- 
ing the religion of the Sumerians these royal inscriptions are quite 
as important as the liturgies and hymns. 

The inscriptions contained in this volume are also interesting from 
the literary point of view. In them may be traced the rise and 
decay of a literary feeling and a literary style. In the earliest in- 
scriptions we have only the bare record of objective facts. Even 
when the narrative is of considerable length, as in the inscriptions 
of Ur-Nina and Eannatum, there is but the bare record of facts 
expressed with much repetition. Literary feeling and expression 
improved, however, as time passed and reached their culmination 
in the long and prosperous reign of Gudea. In his longer inscrip- 
tions, and especially in his cylinder inscriptions, we have the cul- 


XXli Introduction 


mination of Sumerian literary feeling and style. These writings 
abound in beautiful and well-expressed comparisons. A German 
scholar! has recently published a study of similes in the Akkadian 
royal inscriptions; an equally fertile field is presented by the Sumerian. 
The scribes of Gudea had closely observed nature; they had watched 
the stars at night, they appreciated the beauties of the moon in mid- 
heaven, the glories of dawn, the flight of birds, the appearance of 
fishes and water-snakes, the glories and majesty of the thunder- 
storm, the bellowing of the bull, the appearance of a wild ox, and 
many other natural phenomena, and adorn their pages—or should 
we say their clay and stones?—with well-expressed comparisons 
drawn from these phenomena. Akkadian scribes during this period 
never approached this literary excellence. Their inscriptions, like 
those of the earliest Sumerian rulers, are expressed in a meager, 
stereotyped vocabulary with many repetitions. After the time of 
Gudea the literary form of royal inscriptions rapidly declined. The 
scribes of Ur, under the last great dynasty which flourished there, 
lacked the genius of those who had flourished at Lagash a little 
earlier. 

The inscriptions of the dynasties of Isin and Larsa reveal in their 
vocabulary and modes of expression new influences. Foreign ele- 
ments were becoming dominant in Sumer and, though scribes still 
wrote in the old sacred tongue, and sometimes rose above the dreary 
monotony of the style of the royal inscription at its worst, one who 
has saturated himself with the Sumerian inscriptions of the earlier 
dynasties quickly detects the foreign element.

1 Strictly speaking the following inscription is not a “royal’’ inscription. It 
has, however, been included in the volume because of its historical importance. 
It records the purchase of a house for the wife of Enlitarzi, who was afterward 
patest of Lagash, and the payments and presents that were made in connection 
with the purchase and the establishment of the family init. Incidentally it gives 
the ancestry of Barnamtarra, the famous wife of Lugalanda, the next patesi after 
Enlitarzi.

‘the Antasurra ‘with fire have destroyed; ‘its silver and lapis 
lazuli ‘they have plundered; *in the temple of Tirash *they

Ebabbar ‘they have shed blood; ‘its gold and lapis lazuli *they 
have plundered; 'in Gzkana,’ of Ninmakh !the sacred grove,

a This is not the ideogram by which the name Umma is usually expressed, but 
we learn from the Cone inscription of Entemena that Urlumma was ruler of 
Umma in his time and that Enakalgi was its ruler in the time of Eannatum. It 
seems safe therefore to assume that this sign is another way of writing the name 
of that city.

15. Cylinder A.! I, ‘When heavenly and earthly fates were 
allotted, *Lagash lifted its head with a great cry to heaven. 
’Enlil cast a favorable look at the lord, Ningirsu, (saying), 
4“Tn my city that gladness which is appointed has not come 
forth; *the inundation does not rise—*the inundation of Enlil 
does not rise—’the inundation does not rise; *high water 
glistens not as it rises; *the inundation of Enlil does not, as the 
Tigris, bring good water. Its king shall name the temple 
NEninnu; his edict he shall make brilliant in heaven and 
earth.” “The patesi, a man of large intellect, directed thereto 
his intellect; “with large presents he drew near, “with appro- 
priate oxen and kids he sought the divine will, the brick of 
destiny he put upon his head, "to build the holy temple he 
made firm its walls. !’To his king by day and in the middle of 
the night '8Gudea lifted up his eyes to his lord Ningirsu. !°He 
commanded him to build his temple: ?°0n Eninnu’s great service, 
*the set his eyes. ?*Gudea, whose heart was deep, *said as he 
sighed: *“‘Thus, thus will I speak; thus, thus will I speak, 
>this word will I bring forth: ?*I am the shepherd; the chief 
lordship is given to me.” ?7At midnight something came to me. 
*8Tts meaning I did not understand. ?°To my mother I brought 
my dream. II, '‘O holy oracle-priestess, who knows what 
befits me, 70 Nina, lady of Sirara, send down to me! ‘ts 
meaning explain!’ ‘In his asphalt-lined boat he set foot *to go

aPrice takes SILIM...MI-SUM to mean “a story he erected,” xxi, 1-12 as the 
making of the seven stages of the ziggurat, and the sentences which I have taken 
as blessings to be the names of its stages. It is true that Gudea several times 
says that he built the Epa, or ziggurat (see Statues D, ii; E, i, 16 ff.; G, i, 11 ff.; 
I, ii, 11 and 12; and Clay Cone C, 11), and unless this passage refers to its erection 
the details of the building are nowhere given. The word for the stages of a 
ziggurat is, however, UB, not SILIM or SA, and elsewhere, whenever a sentence 
is to be taken as a name we have the phrase Mu-S0 Mu-NA-SA, “with this name 
he named it,” which is wanting here. It seems preferable, with Thureau-Dangin, 
to interpret as we have done.

a Zazaru and Urnuntaea were two of the seven daughters of the goddess Bau 
(see Gudea, Cylinder B, xi, 4-11). Urkagina (Cone A, col. ii) as well as Gudea 
mentions them. Both Urkagina and Gudea mention the goddess Impae between 
Zazaru and: Urnuntaea. It is clear from the context that at the beginning of 
col. iii there once stood a statement that the temple of Zazaru was built, but 
we have no way of determining how many lines have been lost. Zazaru may 
have been described by epithets as the other goddesses are, and a statement of 
the building of a temple to Impae may also have intervened.

years, Sumu-ilu; §16 years, Nur-Adad °7 (?) years, Sin-idinam

was king: !7289 are their years.

APPENDIX II 


Since the manuscript of this book went to the printers the 
British Museum and the University Museum in Philadelphia 
have issued a most important work, Ur Excavations, Texts, I, 
edited by C. J. Gadd and Leon Legrain. The work contains 
inscriptions found at Ur and Al-Ubaid, one hundred and forty- 
five of which come from the period covered by the kings of 
Sumer and Akkad. Four or five of these, which the writer had 
been permitted to copy from originals and replicas in the uni- 
versity Museum, are found in their proper places above. A 
considerable number of the remainder are too fragmentary to 
be of value, and the names of the kings for whom they were 
composed are lost. The rest of them (eighty-four in all) are 
transliterated and translated here. In order to save space 
many of the stereotyped titles of the rulers have been omitted. 
The points at which such omissions have been made are indi- 
cated by “‘(etc.)” in the translation, as well as by the sign of 
omission, ‘‘(...),” in the text. It is also indicated by the num- 
bering of the lines. As the texts have all been published and 
translated by Gadd and Legrain, the usual bibliographical notes 
have been omitted, the number in each foot-note referring 
simply to the number which that inscription bears in their work. 
To these one unpublished tablet belonging to Mr. John F. Lewis 
of Philadelphia has been added. 

Of these new inscriptions, three are from rulers from whom 
we had no historical inscriptions before: Utu-khegal of the 
dynasty of Erech, who immediately preceded Ur-Nammu of the 
last dynasty of Ur; Enlil-anishag, a hitherto unknown patesi 
of Nippur; and Silli-Adad (Silli-Immer) of the dynasty of Larsa. 

The remaining inscriptions are distributed among kings al- 
ready represented in the preceding pages as follows: from Enan- 
natum I, one; from the time of Sargon, one; Rimush, one; 
Naram-Sin, one; Ur-Bau, one; Gudea, three; Ur-Nammu, fifteen.

Nippur, ‘prince of heaven and earth, *Gudea, *patesz ‘of Lagash;, 
‘the long boat °of Ekur ‘for his life “presented.

pur, ‘the mighty king, *king of Isin, ‘king of Sumer and Akkad, 
‘spouse chosen of the heart 8of Ininni, *beloved of Enlil !°and 
of Nin-Insina, “the great wall of Isin “built; “that wall 1“‘Enlil- 
'6is-the-foundation-of-the-earth (?)” is its name. 

5. Damigq-ilishu

head %of Nippur, ‘the shepherd favored of Anu, ‘who is given

‘productive husbandman, °the store-house of the whole land, 
‘the faithful nourisher, "the father of the lofty palace, “the 
mighty king, “king of Isin, II, king of Sumer and Akkad, 
*the suitable, faithful one, who lordship *brings to Ininni, ‘the 
great wall ‘of Isin ‘built. That wall, *“Damigq-ilishu- °is-the- 
favorite-of-Nin-urta” !is its name.
